From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia
Concept considered fundamental to Buddhist ethics
For the concept of merit in Hinduism, see [Punya (Hinduism)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punya_(Hinduism)).
Merit, demerit and its retributions at the level of the individual. Based on Spiro. [\[1\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpiro1982141-1)
**Merit** ( [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit_language): *puṇya* ; [Pali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pali_language): *puñña* ) is a concept considered fundamental to [Buddhist ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_ethics). It is a beneficial and protective force which accumulates as a result of good deeds, acts, or thoughts. **Merit-making** is important to Buddhist practice: merit brings good and agreeable results, determines the quality of the [next life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebirth_(Buddhism)) and contributes to a person's growth towards [enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enlightenment_in_Buddhism). In addition, merit is also shared with a deceased loved one, in order to help the deceased in their new existence. Despite [modernization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_modernism), merit-making remains essential in traditional Buddhist countries and has had a significant impact on the rural economies in these countries.
Merit is connected with the notions of [purity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purity_in_Buddhism) and goodness. Before Buddhism, merit was used with regard to [ancestor worship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancestor_worship), but in Buddhism it gained a more general ethical meaning. Merit is a force that results from good deeds done; it is capable of attracting good circumstances in a person's life, as well as improving the person's mind and inner well-being. Moreover, it affects the next lives to come, as well as [the destination a person is reborn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_cosmology). The opposite of merit is [demerit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_views_on_sin) ( *pāpa* ), and it is believed that merit is able to weaken demerit. Indeed, merit has even been connected to the path to [Nirvana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirvana_(Buddhism)) itself, but many scholars say that this refers only to some types of merit.
Merit can be gained in a number of ways, such as [giving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dana_(Buddhism)), [virtue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9A%C4%ABla) and [mental development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhavana). In addition, there are many forms of merit-making described in [ancient Buddhist texts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_texts). A similar concept of *kusala* ( *Sanskrit: kuśala* ) is also known, which is different from merit in some details. The most fruitful form of merit-making is those good deeds done with regard to the [Triple Gem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triple_Gem), that is, the [Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddha), his teachings, the [Dhamma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharma) ( *Sanskrit: Dharma* ), and the [Sangha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangha). In Buddhist societies, a great variety of practices involving merit-making has grown throughout the centuries, sometimes involving great self-sacrifice. Merit has become part of [rituals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_liturgy), [daily and weekly practice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_practice), and [festivals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Buddhism). In addition, there is a widespread custom of [transferring merit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pari%E1%B9%87%C4%81man%C4%81) to one's deceased relatives, of which the origin is still a matter of scholarly debate. Merit has been that important in Buddhist societies, that [kingship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_kingship) was often [legitimated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legitimacy_(political)) through it, and still is.
In modern society, merit-making has been criticized as [materialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiritual_materialism), but merit-making is still ubiquitous in many societies. Examples of the impact of beliefs about merit-making can be seen in the *Phu Mi Bun* rebellions which took place in the last centuries, as well as in the revival of certain forms of merit-making, such as the much discussed [merit release](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_release).

#### Definition

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=1)]

| Translations of *Merit* |  |
| --- | --- |
| [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit) | *puṇya* |
| [Pali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pali) | *puñña* |
| [Burmese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burmese_language) | ကောင်းမှု ( [MLCTS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MLC_Transcription_System): *káʊ̃ m̥ṵ* ) |
| [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language) | 功德 ( [Pinyin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinyin): *gōng dé* ) |
| [Japanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_language) | 功德 (くどく) ( [Rōmaji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C5%8Dmaji): *kudoku* ) |
| [Khmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmer_language) | បុណ្យ (bon) |
| [Lao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lao_language) | ບຸນ (bun) |
| [Tagalog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagalog_language) | *puna* |
| [Tibetan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Tibetan) | བསོད་ནམས ( *bsod nams* ) |
| [Thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_language) | บุญ [\[būn\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Thai) ( [RTGS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Thai_General_System_of_Transcription): *bun* ) |
| [Vietnamese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnamese_language) | *công đức phước* |
| [Glossary of Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_Buddhism) |  |

*Puñña* literally translates as 'merit, meritorious action, virtue'. [\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTERhys_DavidsStede192186-2) It is glossed by the [Theravāda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Therav%C4%81da) Commentator [Dhammapāla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhammapala) as " *santanaṃ punāti visodheti* ", meaning 'it cleans or purifies the life-continuity'. [\[3\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003461-3) [\[4\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201244-4) Its opposites are [apuñña (demerit) or pāpa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_views_on_sin) ('infertile, barren, harmful, bringing ill fortune'), [\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTERhys_DavidsStede192186-2) [\[4\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201244-4) [\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTENyanatiloka1980b-5) of which the term *pāpa* has become most common. [\[3\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003461-3) The term *merit* , originally [a Christian term](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Catholic_Church)), has in the latter part of the twentieth century gradually been used as a translation of the Buddhist term *puñña* or *puñña* . [\[6\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPyeStrong19875870,_5873-6) The Buddhist term has, however, more of an impermanent character than the English translation implies, [\[7\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHanks19621247-7) and the Buddhist term does not imply a sense of *deserving* . [\[8\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200018-8) [\[9\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTECousins1996155-9)
Before the arising of Buddhism, merit was commonly used in the context of [Brahmanical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmanical) sacrifice, and it was believed that merit accrued through such sacrifice would bring the devotee to an eternal heaven of the ['fathers'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pit%E1%B9%9Bs) ( *Sanskrit: pitṛ, pitara* ). [\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolt1981-10) [\[11\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003457%E2%80%938-11) [\[12\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPremasiri197666-12) However, the Vedic words for merit also carried ethical meaning. [\[13\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-13) Later, in the period of the [Upaniśads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upanishads), a concept of rebirth was established and it was believed that life in heaven was determined by the merit accumulated in previous lives, [\[14\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-14) [\[11\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003457%E2%80%938-11) [\[12\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPremasiri197666-12) but the focus on the *pitṛ* did not really change. [\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolt1981-10) In Buddhism, the idea of an eternal heaven was rejected, but it was believed that merit could help achieve a rebirth in a temporary heaven. [\[11\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003457%E2%80%938-11) Merit was no longer merely a product of ritual, but was given a new ethical meaning. [\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-15)
In the [Tipiṭaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tipi%E1%B9%ADaka) ( *Sanskrit: Tripiṭaka* ; the Buddhist scriptures), the importance of merit is often stressed. Merit is generally considered fundamental to [Buddhist ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_ethics), in nearly all Buddhist traditions. [\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTENyanatiloka1980b-5) [\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTETanabe2004532-16) [\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcFarlane1997409-17) Merit-making is very important to Buddhist practice in Buddhist societies. [\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Fuller_2016-18) [\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003-19) [\[20\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBasham1989126-20)
Merit is a "beneficial and protective force which extends over a long period of time" (B.J. Terwiel)—and is the effect of good deeds ( *Pali: kamma* , *Sanskrit: karma* ) done through physical action, words, or thought. [\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Terwiel_1976-21) [\[22\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEgge201321-22) [\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGutschow200414-23) As its [Pāḷi language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81li_language) (the language of Theravāda Buddhism, as practiced in Sri Lanka, Thailand, Myanmar, etc.) definition indicates, this force is associated with goodness and purity of mind. [\[24\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich200944-24) In traditional Buddhist societies, it is believed that merit is more sustainable than that of magical rites, spirit worship or worldly power. [\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHanks19621254-25) The way merit works, is that acts of merit bring good and agreeable results, whereas demeritorious acts bring bad and disagreeable results. A mixture of the two generates mixed results in a person's life. This [karmic correspondence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karma_(buddhism)) ( *Pali: kamma-sarikkhatā* ) or "automatic cosmic reaction" (Brokaw) is a common idea found in [Buddhist texts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_texts) and Buddhist societies, [\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003-19) [\[26\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrokaw201428-26) and explains why people are different and lead different lives in many ways. [\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Fuller_2016-18) [\[27\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeyes197396-27) Karma is self-regulatory and natural: it operates without divine intervention and human intention is fundamental to it. [\[8\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200018-8) [\[6\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPyeStrong19875870,_5873-6) [\[28\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich1971204%E2%80%935-28)
Internally, merit makes the mind happy and virtuous. [\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-KRC_2005-29) [\[30\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeyes1983268-30) [\[31\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTECateLefferts2006589-31) Externally, present good circumstances, such as a long life, health and wealth, as well as the character and abilities someone is born with, arise from merits done in the past and vice versa, with demerits. [\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Terwiel_1976-21) [\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott200929-32) [\[33\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams2008158-33) The merits and demerits a person has done may take a while to bear fruit. [\[34\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Rao_Paranjpe_2015-34) Merit or demerit may cause a good or bad future respectively, including in the next lives to come. [\[6\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPyeStrong19875870,_5873-6) [\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott200929-32) A bad destination after rebirth may be caused by demerit, but merely a lack of merit may also lead a person to be born in an unhappy destination. [\[35\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-35) When someone is reborn in a happy destination, however, one can only stay there as long as merits last. [\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGutschow20042-36) Thus, it is stated in the Tipiṭaka that people cannot take anything with them when they die, except for whatever merit and demerit they have done, which will affect their future. [\[37\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-37) [\[38\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003460-38) [\[39\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200019-39) Merit can be accumulated in different quantities, and stored up, but also has an impermanent character: it can run out. [\[22\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEgge201321-22) [\[40\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHanks1962-40) [\[41\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeyes1983267%E2%80%938-41) Summarizing from the Buddhist text [Milinda Pañhā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milinda_Panha), some scholars conclude that merit is inherently stronger than demerit. [\[42\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003471-42) [\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200020-43) Moreover, many merits together have the power to prevent demerits from having an effect, by pushing them "to the back of the queue" ( [Richard Gombrich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Gombrich)), though demerits can never be undone. [\[44\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Langer_2007-44) [\[45\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich2006127-45) [\[46\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGutschow200415-46)
All these benefits of merit ( *Pali: ānisaṁsa* ; *Sanskrit: ānuśaṁsa* ), whether internal or external, are the aim in merit-making, and are often subject of Dharma teachings and texts. [\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Jory_1996-47) [\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESkilling20059832%E2%80%933-48) Thus, merit is the foundation of heavenly bliss in the future, [\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTERhys_DavidsStede192186-2) and in some countries merit was also considered to contribute to the good fortune of the country. [\[49\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESalguero2013342-49) [\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTECateLefferts2006590-50) Because merit is understood to have these many beneficial effects, it is sometimes compared with cool water, which is poured or which is bathed in. This symbol is used in merit transfer ceremonies, for example. [\[51\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Calkowski_2006b-51) [\[52\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-52)

#### Discussion in traditional texts

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=2)]

##### General

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=3)]
Merit is not only a concept, but also a way of living. [\[53\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMulder1969109-53) The [Pāli canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81li_canon) identifies three bases of merit ( *puññakiriyā-vatthu* ), [\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTERhys_DavidsStede192186-2) [\[38\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003460-38) [\[39\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200019-39) in order of difficulty: [\[54\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMulder1969109%E2%80%9310-54) [\[note1\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-56)
\* giving ( *dāna-maya* )
\* virtue ( *sīla-maya* )
\* mental development ( *bhāvanā-maya* )
In Buddhist texts and practice, giving is considered the easiest of the three bases of merit. [\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFindly2003185,_250-57) It helps to overcome selfishness and stills the mind; it prepares the mind for the practice of virtue. [\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcFarlane1997409-17) It is also considered a form of saving, considering there is a rebirth in which people receive back what they have given. [\[57\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-58) As for virtue, this comprises three out of eight aspects of the [Noble Eightfold Path](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noble_Eightfold_Path), the path central in the Buddhist teaching: [right speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_speech), right action and [right livelihood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_livelihood). Being the main criterion for moral behaviour in Buddhism, virtue is mostly about the undertaking of [five precepts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_precepts), [\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcFarlane1997409-17) [\[58\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones1979372-59) although the [eight precepts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eight_precepts) may be kept now and then. [\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTENamchoomLalhmingpuii201647-60) The five precepts are part of many [Buddhist ceremonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_ceremonies), and are also considered a merit itself, helping the practitioner to become strong and healthy. [\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcFarlane1997409-17) [\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones1979374-61) [\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESalguero2013344-62) The benefits of practicing the three bases of merits are also summarised as three forms of happiness ( *Pali: sampatti* )—happiness as a human being, happiness in heaven, and happiness in [Nirvana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirvana). [\[62\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESkilling20059832-63) When people die, what world they will be reborn into depends on how intense they practice these three bases of merit. It is, however, only mental development that can take someone to the highest heavenly worlds, or to Nirvana. [\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003460,_462-64)
Giving helps to overcome selfishness and stills the mind; it prepares the mind for the practice of virtue. [\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcFarlane1997409-17)
[Post-canonical texts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pali_literature#List_of_post-canonical_Pali_texts) and [commentaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atthakatha) [\[note2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-65) such as the [Dhammasaṅganī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhammasangani) and [Atthasālinī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atthasalini), [\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003470-66) [\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTENyanatiloka1980c275-67) elaborating on the three bases of merit, state that [lay devotees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upasaka) can make merit by performing ten deeds. Seven items are then added to the previous three:
\* Giving ( *Dāna-maya* )
\* Virtue ( *Sīla-maya* )
\* Mental development ( *Bhāvanā-maya* )
\* Honoring others ( *Apacāyana-maya* )
\* Offering service ( *Veyyāvaca-maya* )
\* Dedicating (or transferring) merit to others (Pāli: *Pattidāna-maya* ; Sanskrit: *puṇyapariṇāmanā* )
\* Rejoicing in others' merit ( *Pattānumodanā-maya* )
\* Listening to Buddha's Teachings ( *Dhammassavana-maya* )
\* Instructing others in the Buddha's Teachings ( *Dhammadesanā-maya* )
\* Straightening one's own views in accordance with the Buddha's Teachings ( *Diṭṭhujukamma* ) [\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTERhys_DavidsStede192186-2) [\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003470-66) [\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPyeStrong19875873-68)
These ten, the Commentator [Buddhaghoṣa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhaghosa) says, all fit within the three first bases of merit: 'Giving' includes 'Transferring merit to others' and 'Rejoicing in others' merit' by extension, whereas 'Virtue' includes 'Honoring others' and 'Offering service'. The remaining items 'Listening to Teachings', 'Instructing others in the Teachings' and 'Straightening one's own views' are part of 'Mental development'. [\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003470-66) Thus, in Theravāda Buddhism, merit is always accrued through morally (good) actions. Such good deeds are also highly valued in the other two Buddhist schools, that is [Mahāyāna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mah%C4%81y%C4%81na) (China, Japan, etc.) and [Vajrayāna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vajray%C4%81na) (Tibet, Nepal, etc.). In some forms of Mahāyāna or Vajrayāna it is believed, however, that even more merit will accrue from certain ritual actions, sometimes called the 'power of blessed substances' *( Standard Tibetan: rdzas)* . These are considered an addition to the traditional list and can help protect against calamities or other negative events caused by bad karma. [\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTETanabe2004532-16) [\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGutschow200414%E2%80%935-69)
A number of scholars have criticized the concepts of merit and karma as amoral, egoist and calculative, citing its quantitative nature and emphasis on personal benefits in observing morality. [\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Jory_1996-47) [\[68\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpiro1982105%E2%80%937-70) [\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeown199213-71) Other scholars have pointed out that in Buddhist ethics egoism and altruism may not be as strictly separated as in western thought, personal benefit and that of the other becoming one as the practitioner progresses on the spiritual path. [\[70\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-72) [\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Harris_2015-73) [\[72\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Perret_1987-74) Buddhist ethics is informed by Buddhist metaphysics, notably, the [not-self doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatta), and therefore some western ethical concepts may not apply. [\[72\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Perret_1987-74) Besides, as Keown notices, moral action would not be possible if it was not preceded by moral concern for others, as is illustrated by the example of the Buddha himself. Such moral concern is also part of the Buddhist path, cultivated through [loving-kindness and the other sublime attitudes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmavihara) ( *Pali: brahamavihāra* ). [\[73\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeown199274%E2%80%936-75)
Merit is a "beneficial and protective force which extends over a long period of time" (Terwiel)—and is the effect of good deeds. [\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Terwiel_1976-21)

##### Accumulation and fruition

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=4)]
In post-canonical and vernacular Pāḷi literature, such as the [Jātaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C4%81taka) stories of the Buddha's previous lives, the [Avadānas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avadana) and Anisaṃsa texts, as well as in many Mahāyāna texts, merit is the main concept. It is regarded as something which can be accumulated throughout different lifetimes in the process of attaining [Buddhahood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhahood), and is also instrumental in attaining it. The *Bodhisatva* intent on accomplishing Buddhahood and bringing other beings across the ocean of suffering, must do so by accumulating all sorts of merits, in this context also called [perfections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81ramit%C4%81) ( *Pali: pāramī* ; *Sanskrit: pāramitā* ). This form of merit-making is always led by a [vow for enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pra%E1%B9%87idh%C4%81na) ( *Pali: panidhāna* ; *Sanskrit: praṇidhāna* ), and an intention to enlighten others as well, as well as the transferring of merits to all living beings to that effect. [\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPyeStrong19875874-76) [\[75\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200845,_57,_59-77) [\[76\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESkilling20059833,_9839-78)
Another aspect of meritorious acts, emphasised more in later literature, is the idea that a single meritorious act done will reap many fruits, as, for example, expressed in the [Vimānavarthu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vimanavatthu). Not only is the quality of people's next rebirth affected by their merits, but also the circumstances in which they are reborn; not only in the next life, but also in adjacent lives after that. Wealth, lifespan, and position are all contingent on merit. [\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott200929-32) [\[33\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams2008158-33)
In Buddhist texts further details are given in what way and to what extent a meritorious deed will bring results: this depends on the spiritual quality of the recipient, the spiritual attitude of the giver, the manner in which one gives and the object given. [\[77\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Heim_2004-79) [\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200020-43) [\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPyeStrong19875873-68) If the recipient is a human, the gift yields more fruits than if the recipient is an animal, but a gift to a [sāmaṇera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samanera) (a young monk), a monk, many monks, and the Buddha yield even more fruits, in ascending order. [\[78\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrekke1998301-80) [\[79\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpiro1982108%E2%80%939-81) If the giver is motivated by greed or other [defilements of the mind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kleshas_(Buddhism)), the merit gained will be much less than if the giver is motivated by loving-kindness or other noble intentions. [\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott200994-82) Even the intention of going to heaven, though in itself not considered wrong, is not seen as lofty as the intention to want to develop and purify the mind. If the recipient is spiritually "not worthy of the gift", the gift will still be meritorious provided the giver's intention is good, and this is also valid the other way around. [\[81\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200019,_21%E2%80%932-83) [\[82\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrekke1998301,_309%E2%80%9310-84) Good thoughts must also be maintained after the good deed is done, as regretting the gift will also decrease the merit. [\[83\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-85)
Whether the giver pronounces a certain wish or intention also affects the meritorious deed, as the power of the merits can be channeled toward a certain purpose. [\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003468-86) [\[85\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalters200323%E2%80%934-87) [\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEG%C3%B3mez200230%E2%80%931-88) [\[note3\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-89) The manner in which people give is also important: whether someone gives respectfully or not, and whether by giving someone is harming anyone. With regard to the size of the gift, a larger gift is usually more meritorious than a smaller one, but purity of mind affects merit more than the gift's size. [\[87\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200020%E2%80%931,_192-90) It is therefore recommended to give as much as you can afford, no more and no less. [\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpiro1982110-91) Such care in choosing whom to give to and how to give, is called being 'skilled in merit' ( *Pali: puññassa kovidā* ). [\[89\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFindly2003260-92)

##### *Puñña* , *kusala* and Nirvana

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=5)]
See also: [Nirvana (Buddhism)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirvana_(Buddhism))
A teaching that exists in both Mahāyāna *sūtras* and Theravādin *suttas* is the teaching on the Ten Wholesome Ways of Action ( [Pali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pali_language): *kusaladhamma* , *Sanskrit: kuśaladharma* ). In Mahāyāna, this teaching is described as the way in which a *Bodhisattva* prevents "suffering in all [evil destinies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niraya)". These ten wholesome ways are:
\* In giving up the taking of life, the practitioner will accomplish freedom from vexations;
\* In giving up stealing, the practitioner will find security in life, economically, socially and spiritually;
\* In giving up wrongful (sexual) conduct, the practitioner will find inner peace and peace in the family life;
\* In giving up lying, the practitioner will attain purity of speech and mind;
\* In giving up slander, the practitioner will be protected socially and spiritually;
\* In giving up harsh language, the practitioner's words will be more effective;
\* In giving up frivolous speech, the practitioner will become wise and dignified;
\* In giving up lust, the practitioner finds freedom in life through contentment and simplicity;
\* In giving up hatred, the practitioner will develop kindness and gentleness;
\* In giving up wrong views, the practitioner will not falter in the good and spiritual path. [\[90\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-93) [\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-94)
These ten actions are described as *akuśala* ('unwholesome'), and when abstaining from them it is called *kuśala* ('wholesome'). [\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003459,_464-95) [\[note4\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-97) Moreover, *kuśala* andakuśala \*\* are depicted as having 'roots' ( *mūla* ). *Akuśalamūla* are the roots of evil in the mind (the [defilements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kilesa)), whereas the *kuśalamūla* are roots connected with good qualities of the mind. Both of them are called *roots* because they are qualities that can be cultivated and grown in the mind. [\[94\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEG%C3%B3mez2002324%E2%80%935-98) [\[95\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas1953120-99)
*Puṇya* and *pāpa* are close in meaning to *kuśala* and *akuśala* . Both pairs are used for distinguishing between ethically right and wrong. However, even though the negatives *akuśala* and *pāpa* have almost the same meaning, there are some differences between the positives, *kuśala* and *puṇya* . According to [P. D. Premasiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P._D._Premasiri), *Kuśala* is used to describe a more direct path to Nirvana than *puṇya* . [\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPremasiri1976-100) [\[97\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003463%E2%80%935-101) [Damien Keown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damien_Keown), however, believes they are merely different angles of the same concept: *kuśala* refers to the moral status of an action, whereas *puṇya* refers to the experience of the consequences of the action. [\[98\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeown1992123-102) He further points out that in the Pāḷi *suttas* (discourses) mental development ( *bhāvanā* ) practices such as meditation are also included in the path of merit. It is unlikely that in the Tipiṭaka meditation would be regarded as an indirect path or obstacle to Nirvana, [\[99\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003467-103) [\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeown199290-104) and there are passages that directly relate merit to Nirvana. [\[101\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEgge201355%E2%80%936-105) [\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTECollins1997290-106) Sometimes a distinction is made between worldly ( *Pali: lokīya* ) and transcendental ( *Pali: lokuttara* ) merit, in which only transcendental merit leads to liberation. [\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpiro198295-107) [\[104\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTECollins1997482-108)
The Thai scholar and monastic [Phra Payutto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prayudh_Payutto) believes that *merit* and *kuśala* are both used to describe the 'cleanliness of the mind' ( [RTGS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Thai_General_System_of_Transcription): *khwam sa-at mot chot* ). But whereas *merit* aims for the 'beautiful and praiseworthy' ( [RTGS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Thai_General_System_of_Transcription): *suai-ngam na chuenchom* ) aspect of such cleanliness, with worldly benefits such as wealth, praise and happiness; *kuśala* aims for the 'purity' ( [RTGS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Thai_General_System_of_Transcription): *borisut* ) aspect of cleanliness, with enlightenment as its benefit. Phra Payutto does add that both need to be accumulated on the Buddhist path. In making this comparison, he says this only holds for worldly merit, not for transcendental merit. Collins equates transcendental merit with *kusala* . [\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTECollins1997495-109) [\[106\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Payutto_1993-110) In the earlier Pāḷi texts, *kusala* was much more commonly used than *puñña* , *puñña* mostly being used in the context of the practice of giving. [\[107\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTECousins1996154%E2%80%935-111)
In a widely quoted theory, [Melford Spiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melford_Spiro) and Winston King have distinguished two forms of Buddhism found in traditional Buddhist societies, "karmatic Buddhism" focused on activities such as merit-making, and "nirvanic Buddhism" which focuses on the liberation from suffering and rebirth. [\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpiro1982-112) In this theory, called the "transcendency thesis" (Keown), Buddhism has two quite separate aims, which are pursued by separate groups, that is, laypeople (karmatic) and monks (nirvanic). This view has, however, been downplayed or criticized by many other scholars, who believe that kammatic practices are in many ways connected to nibbanic practices, and the aims of monks and laypeople cannot be that easily separated. [\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-113) [\[110\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESwearer199510-114) [\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeown199285%E2%80%93105-115)
This transcendency thesis has also been applied to scriptural interpretation. When discussing the path to the attainment of Nirvana, in some passages in the Tipiṭaka merit is rejected. For example, in the Padhāna Sutra, the [Bodhisatta (the Buddha Gotama to be)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodhisatta#Therav%C4%81da_Buddhism) is tempted by [Māra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mara_(demon)) to give up his [self-torture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mortification_of_the_flesh) practices to do meritorious acts instead. The *Bodhisatta* replies that even a bit of merit is no use to him ( *Pali: "anumattenāpi puññena attho mayhaṃ na vijjati"* ). Some scholars, supporting the transcendency thesis, have interpreted this to mean that merit can only lead to happiness and progress within *Saṃsāra* , but does not lead to Nirvana, and must in fact be discarded before attaining Nirvana. [\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPremasiri197668-116) [\[113\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeown199289-117) [\[114\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEgge201323-118) Marasinghe believes, however, that the word *merit* in this passage refers to merit in the pre-Buddhist Brahmanical sense, connected with rituals and sacrifice, and the lay life. [\[115\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003462%E2%80%933-119)
Another example often quoted in this context is the [simile of the raft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yana_(Buddhism)#Teaching_story_and_metaphor), which states that both *dhamma* and *adhamma* should be let go of in order to attain liberation. Whereas the term *adhamma* in the text clearly refers to evil views, the meaning of *dhamma* is subject to different interpretations. Considering that no other similar passage can be found in the Tipiṭaka, Keown believes that only this passage is not enough to base the transcendency thesis on. [\[116\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeown199294%E2%80%93105-120)
In the Pāḷi Canon, an [enlightened person](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arahat) is said to be neutral in terms of karma, that is, the person no longer generates karma, merit, or demerit. [\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTENyanatiloka1980b-5) [\[117\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003466,_471-121) [\[118\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcDermott1975431%E2%80%932-122) Some scholars have interpreted this to mean that an enlightened person attains a state where distinctions between good and evil no longer exist. Other scholars have criticized this as making little sense, considering how the Buddha would normally emphasize ethics. The fact that an enlightened person is neutral in terms of karma, does not mean he is *ethically* neutral. [\[119\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPremasiri197673-123) [\[120\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003465%E2%80%936-124) Indeed, the Buddha is quoted in the Tipiṭaka as saying he is foremost in 'higher morality' ( *adhisīla* ). [\[121\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeown1992113-125) Keown attempts to overcome this problem by proposing that enlightened people are beyond the accumulative experience of good deeds (merit, *puñña* ), since they are already perfected. They therefore do not need to accumulate goodness and the resulting happiness anymore. They no longer need to strive for a happy rebirth in the next life, because they have gone beyond rebirth. Their enlightenment is, however, an *ethical* perfection as well, though this is solely described as *kusala* , not as *puñña* . [\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Goodman_2009-126) [\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeown199290,_124,_127%E2%80%938-127) [\[124\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpiro198243-128)

#### Field of merit

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=6)]
The main concept of the field of merit is that good deeds done towards some recipients accrue more merit than good deeds to other recipients. This is compared with a seed planted in fertile ground, which reaps more and better fruits than in infertile ground. [\[49\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESalguero2013342-49) [\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrekke1998299%E2%80%93300-129)
In pre-Buddhist [Brahmanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmanism), Brahmin priests used to perform *yajña* *s* (sacrifices) and thereby generating merit for the donors who provided gifts for the sacrifice. In Buddhism, it was the Buddhist monk who assumed this role, considered qualified to receive generosity from devotees and thereby generating merit for them. He came to be described as *āhuneyyo* ('worthy of offering'), by analogy with the Brahmanical term *āhavanīya* ('worthy of sacrifice', used in offerings to the ritual fire); and as *dakkhiṇeyyo* ('qualified to accept the offering'), by analogy with the Brahmanical *dakśiṇā* , the sacrificial offering itself. [\[126\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003459-130) [\[127\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEgge201319%E2%80%9320-131) [\[128\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFindly2003217%E2%80%938,_222-132) The Sangha (monastic community) was also described as 'field of merit' ( *Pali: puññakkhetta* ; *Sanskrit: puṇyakṣetra* ). [\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTERhys_DavidsStede192187-133) [\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrekke1998299-134) [\[131\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEgge201320-135)
The difference with the Brahmanical tradition was, according to Marasinghe, that Buddhism did recognize *other* ways of generating merit apart from offerings to the monk, whereas the Brahmanical *yajña* *only* emphasized offerings to the Brahmin priest. That is not to say that such offerings were not important in [early Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-sectarian_Buddhism): giving to the Sangha was the first Buddhist activity which allowed for community participation, and preceded the first rituals in Buddhism. [\[126\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003459-130)
The main concept of the field of merit is that good deeds done towards some recipients accrue more merit than good deeds to other recipients. This is compared with a seed planted in fertile ground which reaps more and better fruits than in infertile ground. [\[49\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESalguero2013342-49) [\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrekke1998299%E2%80%93300-129) [\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdamek2005141-136) The Sangha is described as a field of merit, mostly because the members of the Sangha follow the [Noble Eightfold Path](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noble_Eightfold_Path). But in many texts, the Buddha and the Dhamma, and their representations, are also described as fields of merit. For example, Mahāyāna tradition considers production and reverence of Dharma texts very meritorious—this tradition, sometimes referred to as the "cult of the book" ( [Gregory Schopen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregory_Schopen)), stimulated [the development of print technology in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_influences_on_print_technology). [\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-137) [\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200845,_145,_149-138) [\[135\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-139) In other traditions a Buddha image is also considered a field of merit, and any good deed involving a Buddha image is considered very meritorious. [\[136\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-140) [\[137\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESkilling20059829-141)
A meritorious deed will also be very valuable (and sometimes viewed in terms of a field of merit) if performed to repay gratitude to someone (such as parents), or performed out of compassion for those who suffer. [\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTETanabe2004532-16) [\[138\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESalguero2013345-142) [\[139\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200023-143) Deeds of merit done towards the Sangha as a whole ( *Pali: saṅghadāna* ) yield greater fruits than deeds done towards one particular recipient ( *Pali: pāṭipuggalikā dakkhiṇā* ) or deeds done with favoritism. [\[77\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Heim_2004-79) [\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPyeStrong19875873-68) [\[140\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200022-144) Indeed, *saṅghadāna* yields even more fruits than deeds of merit to the person of the Buddha himself. [\[78\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrekke1998301-80) [\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdamek2005140-145)

#### Practice in Buddhist societies

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=7)]
Thus the Buddhist's view of his present activities has a wider basis, they being but one group of incidents in an indefinitely prolonged past, present and future series. They are, as has been said, no mere train of witnesses for or against him, but a stage in a cumulative force of tremendous power. He and his works stand in a mutual relation, somewhat like that of child to parent in the case of past works, of parent to child in the case of future works. Now no normal mother is indifferent as to whether or how she is carrying out her creative potency. Nor can any normal Buddhist not care whether his acts, wrought up hourly in their effect into his present and future character, are making a happy or a miserable successor. And so, without any definite belief as to how, or in what realm of the universe he will re-arise as that successor to his present self, the pious Buddhist, no less than his pious brethren of other creeds, goes on giving money and effort, time and thought to good works, cheerfully believing that nothing of it can possibly forgo its effect, but that it is all a piling up of merit or creative potency, to result, somewhere, somewhere, somehow, in future happiness—happiness which, though he be altruistic the while, is yet more a future asset of his, than of some one in whom he naturally is less interested than in his present self. He believes that, because of what he is now doing, some one now in process of mental creation by him, and to all intents and purposes his future " self," will one day taste less or more of life's trials. To that embryonic character he is inextricably bound ever making or marring it, and for it he is therefore and thus far responsible.
— [C. A. F. Rhys Davids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._A._F._Rhys_Davids), A Study of the Buddhist Norm [\[142\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-146)

##### Merit-making

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=8)]
The ten bases of merit are very popular in Buddhist countries. [\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTENyanatiloka1980c275-67) In China, other similar lists are also well-known. [\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalsh2007361-147) [\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTELamotte198872-148) In [Thai Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Thailand), the word "merit" ( [RTGS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Thai_General_System_of_Transcription): *bun* ) is often combined with "to do, to make" ( [RTGS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Thai_General_System_of_Transcription): *tham* ), and this expression is frequently used, especially in relation to giving. [\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-149) [\[146\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Seeger-150) [\[147\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMulder1969110-151) [\[note5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-153) In Buddhist societies, such merit-making is common, especially those meritorious deeds which are connected to monks and temples. In this regard, there is a saying in Burma, "Your hands are always close to offering donations". [\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Fuller_2016-18) [\[146\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Seeger-150) [\[149\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarston2006171-154)
Contrary to popular conceptions, merit-making is done by both monastics and laypeople alike. [\[150\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEgge201361-155) [\[151\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMulder1969115-156) [\[152\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchopen1997ix%E2%80%93x,_31-157) Buddhist monks or lay Buddhists earn merit through [mindfulness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mindfulness_(Buddhism)), meditation, chanting and other rituals. Giving is the fundamental way of making merit for many laypeople, as monks are not allowed to cook by themselves. [\[31\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTECateLefferts2006589-31) Monastics in their turn practice themselves to be a good field of merit and make merit by teaching the donors. Merit-making has thus created a symbiotic relationship between laypeople and Sangha, [\[77\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Heim_2004-79) [\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPyeStrong19875873-68) [\[153\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTENamchoomLalhmingpuii201652-158) and the Sangha is obligated to be accessible to laypeople, for them to make merit. [\[154\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFindly2003226-159)
Giving can be done in several ways. Some laypeople offer food, others offer robes and supplies, and others fund ceremonies, build monasteries or persuade a relative to ordain as a monk. Young people often temporary ordain as monks, because they believe this will not only yield fruits of merit for themselves, but also for their parents who have allowed them to ordain. [\[155\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-160) [\[156\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-161) [\[157\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott200995-162) In China, Thailand and India, it used to be common to offer land or the first harvest to a monastery. [\[158\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalsh2007362%E2%80%936-163) [\[153\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTENamchoomLalhmingpuii201652-158) [\[159\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFuengfusakul199853%E2%80%934-164) Also, more socially oriented activities such as building a hospital or bridge, or giving to the poor are included in the Tipiṭaka, and by many Buddhists considered meritorious. [\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPremasiri197668-116) [\[151\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMulder1969115-156) [\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott2009126-165) In fieldwork studies done by researchers, devotees appreciated the merits of becoming ordained and supporting the building of a temple the most. [\[157\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott200995-162) [\[161\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMulder1969-166) Fisher found that building a temple was considered a great merit by devotees, because they believed they would in that way have part in all the wisdom which would be taught at that temple. [\[162\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFisher2008148-167)
People may pursue merit-making for different reasons, as Buddhist orthodoxy allows for various ideals, this-worldly or ultimate. [\[163\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESwearer19956-168) Although many scholars have pointed out that devotees often aim for this-worldly benefits in merit-making, [\[164\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott2009101-169) [\[165\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMulder1979127-170) it has also been pointed out that in old age, people tend to make merit with a view on the next life and liberation. [\[165\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMulder1979127-170) [\[166\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESwearer199522-171) Among lay people, women tend to engage in merit-making more than men, and this may be a way for them to enhance [empowerment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empowerment). [\[31\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTECateLefferts2006589-31) [\[167\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBao2005124-172) [\[168\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarston2006172-173) Very often, merit-making is done as a group, and it is believed that such shared merit-making will cause people to be born together in next lives. This belief holds for families, friends, communities and even the country as a whole. [\[169\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAppleton2014127,_135%E2%80%936-174) [\[170\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalters200320%E2%80%932-175)
In some cases, merit-making took the form of a community-wide competition, in which different donors tried to outdo each other to prove their generosity and social status. This was the case during merit-making festivals in nineteenth-century Thailand. [\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Fuller_2016-18) [\[171\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJory200261-176) In modern Thailand, businesses and politicians often make merit to improve their public image and increase confidence among customers or voters. [\[172\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFuengfusakul199854%E2%80%935-177) In Burma, lay devotees form associations to engage in merit-making as a community. [\[173\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchober1996passim.-178)
In Thailand and Laos, a yearly festival is held, focused on the [Vessantara Jātaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vessantara_Jataka), a story of a previous life of the Buddha which is held sacred. [\[174\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJory200237-179)
People were so intent on merit-making and giving, that in some societies, people would even offer *themselves* and their family to a Buddhist temple, as one high-ranking minister did in the ancient [Pagan Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pagan_Kingdom) (ninth until fourteenth century Burma). [\[175\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAung-ThwinAung-Thwin201384-180) [\[176\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBowie201719-181) On a similar note, in Sri Lanka, kings and commoners would offer slaves to the temple, and then donate money to pay for their freedom, that way accruing two merits at once. Even more symbolically, kings would sometimes offer their kingdom to a temple, which, returned the gift immediately, together with some Dhamma teaching. Also in Sri Lanka, [King Mahakuli Mahatissa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahakuli_Mahatissa_of_Anuradhapura) disguised himself as a peasant and started to earn his living working on a paddy field, so he would be able to gain more merit by working himself to obtain resources to give to Buddhist monks. [\[177\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTER%C4%81hula1966255%E2%80%936-182)
In some cases, merit-making was even continued after a person's death: in ancient Thai tradition, it was considered meritorious for people to dedicate their corpses to feed the wild animals after death. [\[178\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-183)

##### Rituals

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=9)]
See also: [Buddhist culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_culture) and [Buddhist devotion § Festivals and observance days](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_devotion#Festivals_and_observance_days)
Many devout Buddhists observe regular ["rest days"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uposatha) ( *Pali: uposatha, Sanskrit: upavasatha* ) by keeping five precepts, listening to teachings, practicing meditation and living at the temple. [\[179\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-184) [\[180\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-185) Besides these weekly observances, ceremonies and festivities are yearly held and are often occasions to make merit, [\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-KRC_2005-29) [\[181\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Swearer_1987-186) and are sometimes believed to yield greater merits than other, ordinary days. [\[182\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-187) In Thailand and Laos, a yearly festival ( [RTGS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Thai_General_System_of_Transcription): *Thet Mahachat* ) is held focused on the [Vessantara Jātaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vessantara_Jataka), a story of a previous life of the Buddha which is held sacred. [\[181\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Swearer_1987-186) [\[174\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJory200237-179) This festival, seven centuries old, played a major role in [legitimating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legitimacy_(political)) kingship in Thai society. (see [§ Kingship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#Kingship), below) Making merit is the central theme of the festival. Since the period of [Rama IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rama_IV), however, the festival has become less popular. [\[183\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJory200263%E2%80%934-188) [\[184\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBowie201745-189)
Many countries also celebrate the yearly [Kaṭhina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathina), when they offer robes, money and other requisites to the Sangha as a way to make merit. [\[185\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESwearer199519,_22%E2%80%933-190) [\[186\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTER%C4%81hula1966285-191) In Burma, the two yearly [Light Festivals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tazaungdaing_festival) are typically occasions to make merit, as gifts are given to elders, and robes are sewn for the Sangha. [\[187\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalkowski2006105-192) In South Korea, a [Buddha Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vesak#In_South_Korea) ( [Korean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_language): *seog-ga-tan-sin-il* ) is held, on which Buddhists pray and [offer alms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alms). [\[188\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-193) Other kinds of occasions of merit-making are also upheld. A special form of merit-making less frequently engaged in is going on pilgrimage, which is mostly common in Tibet and Japan. This practice is highly regarded and considered very meritorious. [\[189\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-194) [\[190\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReaderTanabe1998200-195)

##### Recording

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=10)]
In several Buddhist countries, it has been common to record merits done. In China, it was common for many centuries to keep record of someone's meritorious deeds in 'merit ledgers' ( [pinyin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinyin): *gōngguò gé* ). Although a belief in merit and retribution had preceded the merit ledgers by many centuries, during the [Ming dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ming_dynasty), through the ledgers a practice of systematic merit accumulation was established for the first time. The merit ledgers were lists of good deeds and bad deeds, organized in the form of a calendar for users to calculate to what extent they had been practicing good deeds and avoiding bad deeds every day. The ledgers also listed the exact retributions of every number of deeds done, to the detail. Through these ledgers it was believed someone could offset bad karma. [\[191\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrokaw20143%E2%80%934,_31%E2%80%932-196) [\[192\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-197)
In the fourth century [CE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Era), the *Baopuzi* , and in the twelfth century the *Treatise On the Response of the Tao* and the *Ledger of Merit and Demerit of the Taiwei Immortal* introduced the basics of the system of merit ledgers. In the fourteenth century CE, the [Tao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tao) master Zhao Yizhen recommended the use of the ledgers to examine oneself, to bring emotion in harmony with reason. [\[193\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-198) [\[194\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrokaw201431%E2%80%932-199) From the fourth to the sixteenth centuries, many types of ledgers were produced by Buddhist and Tao schools, and the usage of the ledgers grew widespread. [\[195\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrokaw20143%E2%80%934-200) The practice of recording merits has survived in China and Japan until the present day. [\[196\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTETanabe2004533-201)
In Theravāda countries, for example in Burma and Sri Lanka, similar customs have been observed. [\[197\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTERotman2008-202) [\[198\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpiro1982111%E2%80%932,_454-203) In Sri Lanka, a 'book of merit' ( *Pali: puñña-potthaka* , *Sanskrit: puṇyapustaka* ) was sometimes kept by someone for years and read in the last moments of life. This practice was based on the story of [King Duṭṭhagāmaṇi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutthagamani_Abhaya), and was mostly practiced by the royalty and rich during the period of the [Mahāvaṁsa chronicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahavamsa). [\[199\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTELamotte1988430%E2%80%931-204) [\[200\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich2006140-205) [\[201\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTER%C4%81hula1966xxii%E2%80%93iii,_254%E2%80%935-206) More recent practice has also been observed, for example, as a form of [terminal care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terminal_care). [\[202\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-207) [\[203\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Schlieter_2013-208) or as part of the activities of lay merit-making associations. [\[204\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchober1996205-209)

##### Merit and wealth

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=11)]
The association of wealth with merits done has deeply affected many Buddhist countries. The relation between giving and wealth is ubiquitous in vernacular Pāli literature, and many stories of exemplary donors exist, such as the stories of [Anāthapiṇḍika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anathapindika) and Jōtika. [\[205\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott200930%E2%80%932,_97-210) In Buddhism, by emphasizing the usage of wealth for generosity, accumulating wealth for giving purposes thus became a spiritual practice. [\[206\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFindly20032-211) But using wealth in unrighteous ways, or hoarding it instead of sharing and giving it, is condemned extensively. *Taṇhā* (thirst, desire, greed, craving) is what keeps a person wandering in [Saṃsāra (the cycle of rebirth)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sa%E1%B9%83s%C4%81ra), instead of becoming liberated. It is the attachment to wealth that is an obstacle on the spiritual path, not wealth per se. Stories illustrating these themes in vernacular Buddhist literature, have profoundly influenced [popular culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_culture) in Buddhist countries. [\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Goodman_2009-126) [\[207\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-212) [\[205\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott200930%E2%80%932,_97-210)
Several scholars have described merit as a sort of spiritual currency or bookkeeping system. [\[44\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Langer_2007-44) [\[197\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTERotman2008-202) [\[208\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpiro1982111-213) Though objections have been made against this metaphor, [\[203\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Schlieter_2013-208) [\[209\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeyes198318%E2%80%939-214) it is not new. Similar comparisons have been made in the [Milinda Pañhā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milinda_Panha), and in seventeenth-century China. Moreover, Schopen has shown that Buddhism has had strong connections with the mercantile class, and Rotman thinks that a mercantile ethos may have informed Buddhist texts such as the [Divyāvadāna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divyavadana). [\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTECollins1997290-106) [\[197\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTERotman2008-202) Gombrich objects to calling merit-making "dry metaphysical mercantilism", but he does speculate on a historical relation between the concept of merit and the monetization of ancient India's economy. [\[210\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich2006127%E2%80%938-215)

#### Transfer

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=12)]
Main article: [Transfer of merit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transfer_of_merit)
Sometimes transferring merit is symbolized by pouring water into a vessel. [\[51\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Calkowski_2006b-51) [\[211\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich1971208-216)

##### Description and origins

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=13)]
Two practices mentioned in the list of meritorious acts have been studied quite extensively by scholars: dedicating (or transferring) merit to others, and rejoicing in others' merits. [\[212\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGethin1998109%E2%80%9310-217) Transferring merit is a widespread custom in all Buddhist countries, Mahāyāna, Vajrayāna and Theravāda. [\[213\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Schumann_1973-218) [\[214\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams2008203-219) [\[215\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeyes1977287-220) In the Pāḷi tradition, the word *pattidāna* is used, meaning 'giving of the acquired'. [\[216\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTENyanatiloka1980a-221) And in the Sanskrit tradition, the word *pariṇāmanā* is used for transferring merit, meaning 'bending round or towards, transfer, dedication'. [\[217\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003472-222) Of these translations, 'transfer of merit' has become commonplace, though objected to by some scholars. [\[218\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Masefield_2004-223) [\[219\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich2006126-224)
Buddhist traditions provide detailed descriptions of how this transfer proceeds. Transferring merit to another person, usually deceased relatives, is simply done by a mental wish. Despite the word *transfer* , the merit of the giver is in no way decreased during such an act, just like a candle is used to light another candle, but the light does not diminish. [\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003470-66) [\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPyeStrong19875874-76) [\[220\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMalalasekera196785-225) The merit transferred cannot always be received, however. The dead relatives must also be able to sympathize with the meritorious act. If the relatives do not receive the merit, the act of transferring merit will still be beneficial for the giver himself. The transfer of merit is thus connected with the idea of rejoicing. [\[221\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich1971209%E2%80%9310-226) The other person who rejoices in one's meritorious deeds, in that way also receives merit, if he approves of the merit done. Thus, rejoicing in others' merits, apart from being one of the ten meritorious acts mentioned, is also a prerequisite for the transferring of merit to occur. [\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey200020-43) [\[220\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMalalasekera196785-225) [\[222\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201245-227)
The purposes for merit transfer differ. In many Buddhist countries, transferring merit is connected to the notion of an [intermediate state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bardo). The merit that is transferred to the deceased will help them to cross over safely to the next rebirth. [\[223\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-228) Some Mahāyāna traditions believe that it can help deceased relatives to attain the [Pure Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pure_Land). [\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTETanabe2004532-16) Another way of transferring merit, apart from helping the deceased, is to dedicate it to the *devas* (deities), since it is believed that these are not able to make merits themselves. In this way it is believed their favor can be obtained. [\[44\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Langer_2007-44) [\[222\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201245-227) [\[224\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich200936-229) Finally, many Buddhists transfer merits to resolve a bond of revenge that may exist between people, as it is believed that someone else's vengefulness may create harm in one's life. [\[196\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTETanabe2004533-201) [\[225\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2000335-230)
Initially in the Western study of Buddhism, some scholars believed that the transfer of merit was a uniquely Mahāyāna practice and that it was developed only at a late period after the historical Buddha. For example, [Heinz Bechert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heinz_Bechert) dated the Buddhist doctrine of transfer of merit in its fully developed form to the period between the fifth and seventh centuries CE. [\[226\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBechert1992note_34,_pp._99%E2%80%93100-231) Scholars perceived that it was discordant with early Buddhist understandings of karma, [\[214\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams2008203-219) [\[226\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBechert1992note_34,_pp._99%E2%80%93100-231) [\[227\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich1971204-232) and noticed that in the [Kathāvatthu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kath%C4%81vatthu) the idea is partly refuted by Theravādins. [\[228\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003469-233) [\[229\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich1971216-234) Other scholars have pointed out that the doctrine of the transfer of merit can be found early in the Theravāda tradition. [\[215\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeyes1977287-220) [\[230\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEgge201396-235) [\[231\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMalalasekera196789-236) Then there also scholars who propose that, although the transfer of merit did not exist *as such* in early Buddhism, early doctrines did form a basis for it, the transfer of merit being an "inherent consequence" (Bechert) of these early doctrines. [\[232\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-237) [\[233\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich1971210-238) [\[234\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBechert1992105-239)
The idea that a certain power could be transferred from one to another was known before the arising of Buddhism. In religious texts such as the [Mahābhārata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mah%C4%81bh%C4%81rata), it is described that *devas* can transfer certain powers ( *Sanskrit: tejas* ). A similar belief existed with regard to the energy gained by [performing austerities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asceticism#Indian_religions) ( *Sanskrit: tapas* ). Apart from these transfers of power, a second origin is found in [Brahamanical ancestor worship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veneration_of_the_dead#India). [\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPyeStrong19875874-76) In the period preceding the arising of Buddhism, it was believed that after a person's death he had to be transformed from a wandering *preta* to reach the blissful world of the *pitṛs.* This was done through the complex *Śrāddha* ceremonies, which would secure the destiny of the deceased as a *pitṛ* . In Buddhism, however, ancestor worship was discontinued, as it was believed that the dead would not reach heavenly bliss through rituals or worship, but only through the law of karma. Nevertheless, the practice of transfer of merit arose by using the ethical and psychological principles of karma and merit, and connect these with the sense of responsibility towards one's parents. This sense of responsibility was typical for pre-Buddhist practices of ancestor worship. As for the veneration of dead ancestors, this was replaced by veneration of the Sangha. [\[235\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolt19815%E2%80%9310,_17,_19%E2%80%9320-240) [\[236\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBechert199299%E2%80%93100-241)

##### Application in the spreading of Buddhism

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=14)]
