Sree Padma and Anthony Barber note that merit transfer was well-established and a very integral part of Buddhist practice in the [Andhra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andhra_Pradesh) region of southern India. [\[237\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPadmaBarber2009116-242) In addition, inscriptions at numerous sites across South Asia provide definitive evidence that the transfer of merit was widely practiced in the first few centuries CE. [\[238\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-243) [\[239\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-244) In Theravāda Buddhism, it has become customary for donors to share merits during ceremonies held at intervals, and during a teaching. [\[240\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-245) [\[241\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-246) [\[222\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey201245-227) In Mahāyāna Buddhism, it is believed that *Bodhisattvas* in the heavens are capable of transferring merits, and will do so to help relief the suffering of their devotees, who then can dedicate it to others. This concept has led to several Buddhist traditions focused on devotion. [\[242\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-247) [\[243\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-248) [\[244\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPyeStrong19875874%E2%80%935-249) Mahāyāna and Vajrayāna Buddhists transfer merits as part of the 'Seven-part-worship' ( *Sanskrit: saptāṇgapūjā* ), [\[245\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-250) [\[246\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTELamotte1988433-251) [\[247\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas1953196-252) [\[note6\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-254) and there is almost no ceremony without some form of merit transfer. [\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGutschow200414-23) [\[249\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEG%C3%B3mez2002293-255) Thus, merit transfer has developed to become a standard element in the basic [liturgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_liturgy) of all main schools of Buddhism. Indeed, the transfer of merits has grown that important in Buddhism, that it has become a major way for Buddhism to sustain itself. [\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTETanabe2004532-16) In Japan, some temples are even called *ekōdera* , which means a temple for merit transfer. [\[250\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReaderTanabe199885-256)

#### Kingship

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=15)]
See also: [Buddhist kingship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_kingship)
The [emperor Aśoka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashoka) is featured in later [Pāḷi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81li) works as an important patron supporting the [Sangha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangha). [\[251\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESalguero2013346-257)
In South and Southeast Asia, merit-making was not only a practice for the mass, but was also practiced by the higher echelons of society. Kingship and merit-making went together. [\[175\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAung-ThwinAung-Thwin201384-180) [\[252\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott200998%E2%80%93102-258) In the Tipiṭaka, ideas about good governance were framed in terms of the ideal of the ' [wheel-turning monarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cakravartin)' ( *Pali: Cakkavatti* ; *Sanskrit: Cakravartin* ), the king who rules righteously and non-violently according to [Dharma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharma#Eusebeia_and_dharma). [\[253\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2000114%E2%80%935-259) His roles and duties are discussed extensively in Buddhist texts. The *Cakkavatti* is a moral example to the people and possesses enough spiritual merit. It is through this that he earns his sovereignty, as opposed to merely inheriting it. [\[254\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-260) [\[255\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2000115-261) Also, the Buddha himself was born as a prince, and was also a king (Vessantara) in a previous life. [\[215\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeyes1977287-220) [\[256\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTER%C4%81hula1966256-262) [\[257\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJory2002-263)
Apart from the models in the *suttas* , Pāli chronicles such as the Mahāvaṃsa and the [Jinakālamālī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jinakalamali) may have contributed to the ideals of Buddhist kingship. In these vernacular Pāḷi works, examples are given of royalty performing meritorious acts, sometimes as a form of repentance for previously committed wrongdoings. The emperor [Aśoka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashoka) is featured as an important patron supporting the Sangha. [\[251\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESalguero2013346-257)
Because of these traditions, kings have had an important role in maintaining the Sangha, and publicly performed grand acts of merit, as is testified by [epigraphic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigraphic) evidence from South and Southeast Asia. [\[217\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003472-222) [\[252\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott200998%E2%80%93102-258) In Sri Lanka, from the tenth century CE onward, kings have assumed the role of a lay protector of the Sangha, and so have Thai kings, during the [periods of Sukhothai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhothai_period) and [Ayutthaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayutthaya_period) (fourteenth until eighteenth centuries). In fact, a number of kings in Sri Lanka, Thailand, and Burma have described themselves as *bodhisattas* , and [epithets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epithets) and [royal language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_honorifics#Honorific_registers) were established accordingly. [\[175\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAung-ThwinAung-Thwin201384-180) [\[258\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeyes1977288-264) [\[259\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJory200252-265) In short, kingship in traditional Buddhist societies was connected with the Sangha as a field of merit: the king assumed an exemplary role as a donor to the Sangha, and the Sangha legitimated the king as a leader of the state. Both facilitated one another, and both needed each other. [\[260\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2000117-266) In times of famine or other hardship, it was traditionally believed that the king was failing, and the king would typically perform meritorious activities on a grand scale. [\[197\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTERotman2008-202) [\[261\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJory200253-267) [\[262\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAung-ThwinAung-Thwin2013183-268) In this way the king would be able to improve the kingdom's conditions, through his "overflow karma" (Walters). [\[263\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalters200319-269) A similar role was played by queens. [\[264\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Holt_2003-270) The accumulation of merit in past lives has been attributed to kings' rise to power, and is captured in the Burmese and Thai sociological concepts of [hpone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hpone) and [barami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barami) respectively, both of which have endured in the modern era. [\[265\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-:2-271)
In the last seven centuries in Thailand, the Vessantara Jātaka has played a significant role in legitimating kingship in Thailand, through a yearly festival known as the 'Preaching of the Great Life' ( [RTGS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Thai_General_System_of_Transcription): *Thet Mahachat* ). Merit-making and *pāramīs* (doing good deeds, developing good habits to become a Buddha) were greatly emphasized in this festival, through the story about Prince Vessantara's generosity. During the reform period of Rama IV, as Thai Buddhism was [being modernized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_modernism), the festival was dismissed as not reflecting true Buddhism. Its popularity has greatly diminished ever since. Nevertheless, the use of merit-making by the Thai monarchy and government, to solidify their position and create unity in society, has continued until the late twentieth century. [\[266\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJory201620,_181%E2%80%932-272)

#### In modern society

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=16)]
See also: [Buddhist modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_modernism)

##### 19th–early 20th century

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=17)]
Buddhists are not in agreement with regard to the interpretation, role, and importance of merit. The role of merit-making in Buddhism has been discussed throughout Buddhist history, but much more so in the last centuries. In the nineteenth century, during the rise of Buddhist modernism and the Communist regimes, Buddhists in South and Southeast Asia became more critical about merit-making when it became associated with magical practices, privileging, [ritualism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ritualization) and waste of resources. [\[267\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-273) [\[268\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott200990%E2%80%931-274) [\[269\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTECateLefferts2006588-275) [\[note7\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-278) In pre-modern Thailand, a great deal of the funds of temples were derived from the profits of land that were offered to temples by royalty and nobility. During the period of religious reform and administrative centralization in the nineteenth and early twentieth century, however, Thai temples were no longer supported in this manner and had to find other ways to maintain themselves. [\[159\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFuengfusakul199853%E2%80%934-164)
At the beginning of the twentieth century, perspectives of merit-making had changed again, as merit-making was being associated with capitalism and consumerism, which had been rising in South and Southeast Asia. [\[272\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott200990%E2%80%931,_126-279) [\[273\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESkilling20059833-280) Furthermore, in some Buddhist countries, such as Thailand, there is a tendency among teachers and practitioners to dismiss and even revile merit-making in favor of teachings about detachment and attaining Nirvana, for which [L. S. Cousins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L._S._Cousins) has coined the term " *ultimatism* ". [\[274\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-281) [\[275\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-McCargo_2016-282) [\[276\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-283)

##### From 1960s onward

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=18)]
Studies done in the 1960s and 1970s in Thailand, Sri Lanka and Burma showed that a great deal of time, effort and money was invested by people in merit-making, e.g. Spiro described Burma's rural economy as "geared to the overriding goal of the accumulation of wealth as a means of acquiring merit". In some studies done in rural Burma, up to thirty percent of people's income was spent on merit-making. [\[277\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2000192-284) In 2014, when Burma ranked highest on the [World Giving Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Giving_Index) (tied with the United States, and followed by many other Buddhist countries), scholars attributed this to the Burmese habit of merit-making. [\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Fuller_2016-18) [\[278\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-285) Studies done in Thailand, however, showed that in the 1980s merit-making was declining, and a significant group no longer believed in karma—though this was not a majority. [\[279\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBasham1989128%E2%80%939,_133-286) Some scholars disagree with these findings, however, saying that Buddhist practices such as merit-making are still very widespread. [\[273\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESkilling20059833-280) Similar observations have been made about Cambodia and even about Thai people in the United States. [\[280\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKentChandler200813-287) [\[281\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBao2005-288) As for Buddhist "converts" in the west, for example [from the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_the_United_Kingdom), the interest in merit is less than among Asian Buddhists, but they strongly appreciate the generosity and reverence as exhibited by Asian Buddhists. [\[282\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-289) [\[283\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-290)
Average expenses in merit-making per person, in Thailand, 2005. [\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-KRC_2005-29)

##### Discussion by scholars

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=19)]
Some scholars have suggested that merit-making may have affected the economies of Buddhist countries in a negative way, because spending savings on the local temple would prevent consumption and investment and therefore stunt economic growth. Other researchers have disagreed, pointing out that spending resources on a Buddhist temple does stimulate economic growth through the investment in goods for the temple. [\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-KRC_2005-29) [\[284\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2000193-291) [\[note8\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-293) It has also been suggested that even if the economy of Buddhist countries would be better off without merit-making, it would result in an economy that the majority of the population would not prefer. Another criticism often leveled at merit-making in modern times is that it prevents people from using their resources to help the poor and needy. Very often, however, temples do have many social roles in society, and offer help to many groups in society—resources are therefore redistributed widely. [\[286\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2000193%E2%80%934-294) [\[287\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFisher2008passim.-295) Moreover, since merit-making is often done as a community, merit-making may strengthen [social ties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_ties), which Walters calls "sociokarma". [\[288\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFuengfusakul199853,_57Harvey2000193%E2%80%93194Walters20033-296)
Scholars have often connected the notion of karma to the determinism in the [caste system in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste_system_in_India). [\[289\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHanks19621248-297) Just like in the case of karma, some scholars believe that a belief in merit can cause social differences to stay unchanged. This would be the case when the poor, who cannot make much merit, resign to their fate. [\[290\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-298) [\[291\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGutschow200418-299) Other scholars point out that merit can be used to improve social status in the present, as in the case of someone ordaining as a monk for a few years. [\[215\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeyes1977287-220) And vice versa, if someone's social status quickly deteriorates, for example, due to quick changes in the bureaucratic structure, these changes might be justified in Buddhist societies because someone's store of merit is believed to have run out. [\[292\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBasham1989127%E2%80%938-300) Someone's position in society, even in the cosmos, is always subject to the impermanent workings of merit and demerit. In traditional Buddhist societies, quick changes in position, status, or roles are therefore considered part of life, and this insecurity is a motivator in trying to improve the situation through merit-making. [\[293\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHanks19621247%E2%80%938,_1252-301) [\[294\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeyes197397-302) Findly points out that in Buddhist ideals of merit-making, the earned value gained by doing good deeds is more important than the assigned value gained by social status at birth. [\[295\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFindly2003261%E2%80%932-303)

##### *Phu Mi Bun* movements

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=20)]
See also: [Faith in Buddhism § Millenarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faith_in_Buddhism#Millenarianism)
The idea of merit is also at the basis of the *Phu Mi Bun* movements as has been studied in Thailand and other Buddhist societies. *Phu Mi Bun* are people who are considered to have much merit from past lives, whose influence morally affects society at large. [\[296\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey2000261%E2%80%932-304) [\[297\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeyes1983269-305) [\[298\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMulder1979117-306) *Phu Mi Bun* are in many ways similar to people declared *Bodhisattvas* in Buddhist societies, and in fact, the word *Phu Mi Bun* is often used in traditional Thai texts about the previous lives of the Buddha. Besides the example of the king himself, certain monks and *shamans* have assumed this role throughout history. In Thailand, around the turn of the twentieth century, a [millennialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millennialist) movement arose regarding the coming of a *Phu Mi Bun* , to the extent of becoming an insurgency which was suppressed by the government. [\[299\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeyes1977288%E2%80%9390-307) [\[300\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeyes1973101%E2%80%932-308) [\[301\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJory200245%E2%80%936-309) This insurgency became known to Thai historians as the "rebellion of the *Phu Mi Bun* " ( [RTGS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Thai_General_System_of_Transcription): *Kabot Phu Mi Bun* ), commonly known in English as the [Holy Man's Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Man%27s_Rebellion). [\[302\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-310) Several of such rebellions involving *Phu Mi Bun* have taken place in the history of Thai, Laos, Cambodia and Burma. For example, in Cambodia, there were *Phu Mi Bun* –led revolts against the [French control of Cambodia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Protectorate_of_Cambodia). [\[303\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKentChandler20085-311) Lucien Hanks has shown that beliefs pertaining to *Phu Mi Bun* have profoundly affected the way Thai people relate to authority. [\[40\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHanks1962-40) [Indologist Arthur Basham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Llewellyn_Basham), however, believed that in contemporary Thai society the *Phu Mi Bun* is more of a label, and *merit* more of a secular term than a deeply-rooted belief. [\[304\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBasham1989pp._134%E2%80%935_n.1-312)
Merit release is a ritual of releasing animals from captivity, as a way of making merit. [\[305\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Economist_2015-313)

##### Merit release

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=21)]
Main article: [Life release](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_release)
One merit-making practice that has received more scholarly attention since the 1990s is the practice of "merit release". Merit release is a ritual of releasing animals from captivity, as a way to make merit. Merit release is a practice common in many Buddhist societies, and has since the 2010s made a comeback in some societies. [\[305\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Economist_2015-313) Its origins are unclear, but traditionally it is said to originate from the Mahāyāna *Humane King Sutra* , among other sources. [\[306\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Nuwer_2012-314) [\[307\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEShiuStokes2008184-315) It often involves a large number of animals which are released simultaneously, as well as [chanting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_chant), making a resolution, and transfer of merits. [\[308\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-316) [\[309\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEShiuStokes2008186-317) Though the most common practice is the releasing of fish and birds back in nature, there are also other forms: in Tibet, animals are bought from the slaughterhouse to release. [\[310\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDarlington201625_min.-318)
However, the practice has come under criticism by wildlife conservationists and scholars. Studies done in Cambodia, Hong Kong and Taiwan have shown that the practice may not only be fatal for a high percentage of the released animals, but may also affect the survival of [threatened species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Threatened_species), create a black market for wildlife, as well as pose a threat for public hygiene. [\[306\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Nuwer_2012-314) [\[311\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDarlington201630_min.-319) In Thailand, there are cases where animals are captured for the explicit purpose of being sold to be released—often into unsuitable ecosystems. [\[312\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-320) Some Buddhist organizations have responded to this by adjusting their practices, by working together with conservationist organizations to educate people, and even by pushing for new laws controlling the practice. [\[313\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDarlington201629,32%E2%80%9333_min.-321) In 2016 [\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit), the [Society for Conservation Biology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_for_Conservation_Biology) (SCB) started discussing possible solutions with religious communities on how the practice could be adapted. According to the SCB, the communities have generally responded positively. [\[306\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Nuwer_2012-314) [\[314\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-322) In the meantime, in some countries, laws have been issued to control the practice. In Singapore, to limit merit release on [Vesak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vesak) celebrations, people were fined. [\[307\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEShiuStokes2008184-315)
Despite its critics, merit release continues to grow, and has also developed new forms in western countries. In 2016, it was widely reported that the Canada-based Great Enlightenment Buddhist Institute Society (GEBIS) had released 600 pounds (270 kg) of lobsters in the ocean. The release was planned in agreement with local lobster-men. [\[315\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-Ross_2016-323) In the same year, Wendy Cook from Lincoln, United States, bought about 135 rabbits from a farm to raise them under better conditions. The costly release, advertised on Facebook as *The Great Rabbit Liberation of 2016* , was supported by Buddhist monastics from Singapore and the Tibetan tradition, and was based on the idea of merit-making. [\[316\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-324) In a less successful attempt, two Taiwanese Buddhists released crab and lobsters in the sea at [Brighton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brighton), United Kingdom, to make merit. They were fined by the authorities for £15,000 for a wildlife offense that could have significant impact on native species. [\[317\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-325)

#### Notes

[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Merit_(Buddhism)&action=edit&section=23)]
1. **^** In the Sangīti Sutta ("Chanting together discourse," [Digha Nikaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digha_Nikaya) 33), verse 38, [the leading disciple Sāriputta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sariputra) is described as teaching the same triad: *dāna* , *sīla* , *bhāvanā* . [\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalshe1995485-55)
1. **^** See [Digha Nikāya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digha_Nikaya) iii.218
1. **^** [The announcing of a certain intention in reference to the actions someone has done](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacca-kiriya) ( *Pali: saccakiriyā* ) is a common theme in all Indian religion. [\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarasinghe2003468-86)
1. **^** There is some discussion as to the best translation of *kusala* , some preferring 'skilful' or 'intelligent' instead. [\[93\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTECousins1996137%E2%80%938-96)
1. **^** However, the term *merit-making* may also originate from a translation of Pāli terms. In Pāli texts several of such terms were used. [\[148\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFindly2003256%E2%80%937-152)
1. **^** There are also other forms that are practiced, varying from four to eleven parts. [\[248\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTESkilling20059839-253)
1. **^** From the 1980s onward, the communist regimes in Laos and Cambodia no longer viewed Buddhism as an obstacle to the development of the state, and many of the restrictions with regard to Buddhist practice were lifted. [\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTECateLefferts2006590-50) [\[270\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarston2006169-276) In Burma, the former military government approached merit-making practices differently: they justified their [forced labor camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_labor_camp) by citing that the labor there yielded merit. At the same time, [Aung San Suu Kyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aung_San_Suu_Kyi) referred to the struggle for democracy as *meritorious* . [\[271\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalkowski2006106%E2%80%937-277)
1. **^** Since the 2000s, studies in China have shown a growing interest among local government officials to promote merit-making activities, believing it to stimulate local economy. [\[285\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merit_(Buddhism)#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFisher2008152-292)
